\boldsymbol{\tau}_{{}_{{T^{*}{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}},{\mathsf{st}}}}(GL_{n}\times{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}})

is the set
\{(a,b)\in{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}\times{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}\mid\det a=0\;\&\;\det b=0\}
,
an intersection of two divisors. We conclude that any rational function

f\in\Bbbk({\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}\times{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}})
which is regular on

GL_{n}\times{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}
and on
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{{}_{{T^{*}{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}},{\mathsf{st}}}}(GL_{n}\times{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}})

must be a regular function on the whole of
{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}\times{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}
.
This proves the isomorphism
{(T^{*}GL_{n})}^{\blacktriangledown}_{\rho}{\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}T^{*}{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}
.

To prove the second isomorphism of the proposition, let

{\mathfrak{s}}\subseteq{{\check{\mathfrak{g}}}^{*}_{r}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}
be the Kostant slice.
The Whittaker reduction
T^{*}{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}/\!/\!/_{\psi}U=({\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}\times{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}})/\!/\!/_{\psi}U

may be identified with the variety

\{(a,b)\in{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}\times{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}\mid ba\in{\mathfrak{s}}\}
, where we have used
that
\mu_{r}(a,b)=ba
.
The desired isomorphism
{({\mathsf{Wh}}_{GL_{n})}}^{\blacktriangledown}_{\rho}{\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}

T^{*}GL_{n}/\!/\!/_{\psi}U

follows from this description of
T^{*}{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}/\!/\!/_{\psi}U
by mimicing the proof of the
first isomorphism. The proof of the third isomorphism is similar.
∎

Corollary 3.3.2
.

In the case of the ‘universal Coulomb branch’

\mathbf{M}={\mathsf{st}}\oplus{\mathsf{st}}^{*}
there are natural isomorphisms

({\mathsf{st}}\oplus{\mathsf{st}}^{*})^{!}_{G}\cong T^{*}{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}/\!/\!/_{\psi}U,{\kern 5.0pt}\text{resp.}{\kern 5.0pt}{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathsf{st}}\oplus{\mathsf{st}}^{*},G}\cong U_{\psi}\backslash\!\backslash\!\backslash T^{*}{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}/\!/\!/_{\psi}U.

Proof.

The first isomorphism is a consequence of the isomorphism

{({\mathsf{Wh}}_{GL_{n}})}^{\blacktriangledown}_{\rho}\cong T^{*}{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}/\!/\!/_{\psi}U
of the proposition
and the isomorphism of Theorem
3.2.4
(1).
The second isomorphism follows from this since the Coulomb branch

{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbf{M},G}
is obtained from
\mathbf{M}^{!}_{G}
by the Kostant-Whittaker reduction,
by
[
BF
]
.
∎

Remarks 3.3.3
.

{{\kern 5.0pt}\sf{(i)}}\;
The isomorphisms of the corollary have been proved
earlier in
[
BDFRT
, Sect. 5.2]
in a totally different way.

{\;\sf{(ii)}}\;
The function
\Tr
is well defined on the whole of
{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}
.
Therefore, the
{\mathscr{D}}
-module
\Phi_{{\mathsf{st}}}={\mathscr{D}}_{GL_{n}}(\Tr)
generated by the
function
\Tr
is a restriction to

GL_{n}
of the
{\mathscr{D}}
-module
{\mathscr{D}}_{{}_{{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}}}(\Tr)
on
{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}
.
The first isomorphism of the proposition may be viewed as a classical anague
of this.
Observe further that matrix multiplication makes
{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}
a monoid
and convolution with
{\mathscr{D}}_{{}_{{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}}}(\Tr)
is essentially
the functor of Fourier transform of
{\mathscr{D}}
-modules on
{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}}
.
This is closely related to the fact that the
\gamma
-sheaf counterprt
of
\Phi_{{\mathsf{st}}}
appears in the Godement-Jacquet case of symplectic duality.
However, the roles of
G
and
{\check{G}}
in the Godement-Jacquet setting
of symplectic duality and in the isomorphism of the proposition
are swapt, cf. Remark
2.1.5
(i).

4.
Pointwise pure sheaves

We keep the setting of Sect.
1.1

and use the notation
{\mathfrak{t}}=\Lie T
, resp.
{\mathbb{H}}(\text{-})={H_{T}^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}}(\text{-})
and

{{\mathrm{S}}}={\mathbb{H}}({\operatorname{pt}})
. Thus,
{\mathrm{S}}\cong\Bbbk[{\mathfrak{t}}]
, viewed as a graded algebra such that
{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}
is placed in degree
2
.
Let
Q=\Bbbk({\mathfrak{t}})
be the fraction field of
{\mathrm{S}}
and

M\mapsto QM:=Q\otimes_{\mathrm{S}}M

the localization functor

{\mathrm{S}}\mmod\to Q\mmod
.

4.1.

In this subsection we assume, for simplicity, that
X

is connected.
The fixed point
variety
X^{T}
is a disjoint union

X^{T}=\sqcup_{\tau\in{\mathscr{T}}}\,{\mathcal{X}}_{\tau}
of connected components
{\mathcal{X}}_{\tau}
,
where
{\mathscr{T}}
is a finite labeling set.
Let
\epsilon:X^{T}\,\hookrightarrow\,X
, resp.
\epsilon_{\tau}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\tau}\,\hookrightarrow\,X
,
denote the imbedding.
For
\mathcal{G}\in D_{T}(X)
there are canonical morphisms of
A
-modules

(4.1.1)

\textstyle{{\mathbb{H}}(\epsilon^{!}\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ \bigoplus_{\tau\in{\mathscr{T}}}\ {\mathbb{H}}(\epsilon_{\tau}^{!}\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,\epsilon_{!}\,}
\textstyle{\ {\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G})\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,\epsilon^{*}\,}
\textstyle{\ \bigoplus_{\tau\in{\mathscr{T}}}\ {\mathbb{H}}(\epsilon^{*}_{\tau}\mathcal{G})\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ {\mathbb{H}}(\epsilon^{*}\mathcal{G})\ }
\textstyle{Q{\mathbb{H}}(\epsilon^{!}\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{\tau\in{\mathscr{T}}}\ Q{\mathbb{H}}(\epsilon_{\tau}^{!}\mathcal{G})\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,Q\epsilon_{!}\,}
\textstyle{\ Q{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G})\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,Q\epsilon^{*}\,}
\textstyle{\ \bigoplus_{\tau\in{\mathscr{T}}}\ Q{\mathbb{H}}(\epsilon^{*}_{\tau}\mathcal{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ Q{\mathbb{H}}(\epsilon^{*}\mathcal{G})}

By the Localization theorem, the maps in the second row
are isomorphisms.
If
\mathcal{G}
is
!
-pure, resp.
*
-pure, then the
{\mathrm{S}}
-module

{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G})
, as well as

{\mathbb{H}}(\epsilon^{!}\mathcal{G})
,
resp.
{\mathbb{H}}(\epsilon^{*}\mathcal{G})
, is free, see Corollary
4.1.7
below.
Hence, the first, resp. last, two vertical maps
are injective. It follows that the map
\epsilon_{!}
, resp.
\epsilon^{*}
,
in the first row is injective.

It follows from condition S1 of Sect.
1.1
that the algebra
A:={\mathbb{H}}(X)

is concentrated in even degrees and that it
is free as an
{\mathrm{S}}
-module.
Therefore, the restriction map
\epsilon^{*}:A\to{\mathbb{H}}(X^{T})=\bigoplus_{\tau\in{\mathscr{T}}}\ {\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{X}}_{\tau})

is an injective
{\mathrm{S}}
-algebra map which induces a
Q
-algebra isomorphism:

(4.1.2)

Q\epsilon^{*}:\ QA\,{\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}\,\oplus_{\tau\in{\mathscr{T}}}\ Q{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{X}}_{\tau}).

We will identify
QA
with the direct sum in the RHS and view
A
, resp.
{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{X}}_{\tau})
,
as a subalgebra of
QA
, resp.
Q{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{X}}_{\tau})
.
Writing
e_{\tau}
for the unit of the algebra
{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{X}}_{\tau})
, inside
QA

we get a decomposition
1_{A}=\sum_{\tau\in{\mathscr{T}}}\,e_{\tau}
as a sum of orthogonal
idempotents. Therefore, for any
\mathcal{G}\in D_{T}(X)
we obtain isomorphisms
of
QA
-modules

(4.1.3)

e_{\tau}Q{\mathbb{H}}(\epsilon_{\tau}^{!}\mathcal{G})=Q{\mathbb{H}}(\epsilon_{\tau}^{!}\mathcal{G})\ \xrightarrow[{}^{\sim}]{\ Q(\epsilon_{\tau})_{!}\ }\ e_{\tau}Q{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G})\ \xrightarrow[{}^{\sim}]{\ Q\epsilon_{\tau}^{*}\ }\ e_{\tau}Q{\mathbb{H}}(\epsilon_{\tau}^{*}\mathcal{G})=Q{\mathbb{H}}(\epsilon_{\tau}^{*}\mathcal{G}).

Below we fix a closed subset
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
of the form

X^{\prime}=\sqcup_{\mu\in\Lambda^{\prime}}\,X_{\mu}
for some

\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda
.
Let
X_{\lambda}
be an open stratum in
X^{\prime}
and put
X^{\prime}_{<\lambda}:=X^{\prime}\smallsetminus X_{\lambda}
.
We have a diagram

v:X^{\prime}_{<\lambda}\,\hookrightarrow\,X^{\prime}\hookleftarrow X_{\lambda}:u
,
where
v
, resp.
u
, is a closed, resp.
open, imbedding.
The following lemma is standard

Lemma 4.1.4
.

{{\kern 5.0pt}\sf{(i)}}\;
If
\mathcal{G}\in D_{T}^{\text{mix}}(X^{\prime})
is
!
-pure, resp.
*
-pure,
then (
4.1.5
), resp. (
4.1.6
), below is a short
exact sequence of free
{{\mathrm{S}}}
-modules

(4.1.5)

\displaystyle 0\to{\mathbb{H}}(v^{!}\mathcal{G})\xrightarrow{v_{!}}{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G})\xrightarrow{u^{*}}{\mathbb{H}}(u^{*}\mathcal{G})\to 0;

(4.1.6)

\displaystyle 0\to{\mathbb{H}}_{c}(u^{*}\mathcal{G})\xrightarrow{u_{!}}{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G})\xrightarrow{v^{*}}{\mathbb{H}}(v^{*}\mathcal{G})\to 0.

{\;\sf{(ii)}}\;
If
\mathcal{G}_{X}\in D_{T}^{\text{mix}}(X)
is pure and condition S3 of Sect.
1.1

holds then (
4.1.5
) is a short
exact sequence for
\mathcal{G}:=\iota^{*}\mathcal{G}_{X}
, where
\iota:X^{\prime}\,\hookrightarrow\,X
is the closed imbedding.

Proof.

To prove (i) we induct on the number of strata in
X^{\prime}
.
If
\mathcal{G}
is
!
-pure then
{\mathbb{H}}(u_{!}u^{!}\mathcal{G})\cong{\mathbb{H}}(u^{*}\mathcal{G})[-2\dim X_{\lambda}]

is pure and free over
{{\mathrm{S}}}
.
Further,
{\mathbb{H}}(v^{!}\mathcal{G})

is pure and free over
{{\mathrm{S}}}
, by the induction hypothesis.
Therefore, the connecting homomorphisms
in the long exact sequence associated with the distinguished triangle

v_{!}v^{!}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}\to u_{*}u^{*}\mathcal{G}
vanish. This implies that (
4.1.5
)
is an exact sequence.
The proof of (
4.1.6
) is similar.

To prove (ii) consider the following imbeddings

\{x_{\lambda}\}\ \xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{\lambda}}\ X_{\lambda}\ \xrightarrow{u}\ X^{\prime}\ \xrightarrow{\iota}\ X
,
and the corresponding restriction maps

{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G}_{X})\xrightarrow{i^{*}}{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G})\xrightarrow{u^{*}}{\mathbb{H}}(u^{*}\mathcal{G})\xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{\lambda}^{*}}{\mathbb{H}}(\varepsilon_{\lambda}^{*}u^{*}\mathcal{G})={\mathbb{H}}((\iota{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}u{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}\varepsilon_{\lambda})^{*}\mathcal{G}_{X}).

It is known, cf.
[
Gi
, Lemma 3.5]
, that assumption S3
insures that the composite map
{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G}_{X})\to{\mathbb{H}}((\iota{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}u{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}\varepsilon_{\lambda})^{*}\mathcal{G}_{X})

is surjective.
Therefore, the map
{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G})\to{\mathbb{H}}(u^{*}\mathcal{G})
is surjective since the map
\varepsilon_{\lambda}^{*}

is an isomorphism.
∎

Corollary 4.1.7
.

Let
i:X^{\prime}=\sqcup_{\mu}\,X_{\mu}\,\hookrightarrow\,X
be a closed imbedding,
and
j:X\smallsetminus X^{\prime}\,\hookrightarrow\,X
.
If
\mathcal{G}\in D_{T}^{\text{mix}}(X)
is
!
-pure, resp.
*
-pure, then

{\mathbb{H}}(i^{!}\mathcal{G})
, resp.
{\mathbb{H}}(i^{*}\mathcal{G})
, is pure and free over
{\mathrm{S}}
,
and there is a short
exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to{\mathbb{H}}(i^{!}\mathcal{G})\to{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G})\to{\mathbb{H}}(j^{*}\mathcal{G})\to 0,{\kern 5.0pt}\text{resp.}

\displaystyle 0\to{\mathbb{H}}_{c}(j^{!}\mathcal{G})\to{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G})\to{\mathbb{H}}(i^{*}\mathcal{G})\to 0.

A similar result holds in the case of a closed imbedding

Y^{\prime}=\sqcup_{\mu}\,Y_{\mu}\,\hookrightarrow\,Y
.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
4.1.4
by induction on the number of strata in

X^{\prime}
.
∎

We now assume that the open
stratum
X_{\lambda}
in the diagram

v:X^{\prime}_{<\lambda}\,\hookrightarrow\,X^{\prime}\hookleftarrow X_{\lambda}:u

of Lemma
4.1.4
meets
Y
,
i.e
\lambda\in\Sigma
. Then, the set
Y_{\lambda}=Y\cap X_{\lambda}

is open in
Y\cap X^{\prime}
and
x_{\lambda}\in Y_{\lambda}
.
Let
{\mathcal{X}}_{\bullet}
be the connected
component of
X^{T}
that contains the point
x_{\lambda}
.

Lemma 4.1.8
.

We have
{\mathcal{X}}_{\bullet}\cap X^{\prime}_{<\lambda}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

We have a stratification
{\mathcal{X}}_{\bullet}\cap X^{\prime}=\sqcup_{\mu\in\Omega}\,(X_{\mu})^{T}
for some subset
\Omega\subseteq\Lambda
.
Since
x_{\lambda}\in Y^{T}
,
condition S2 of Sect.
1.1

implies that
{\mathcal{X}}_{\bullet}\cap X_{\lambda}=\{x_{\lambda}\}

is the one point stratum of
{\mathcal{X}}_{\bullet}
.
It follows that
x_{\lambda}
is contained in the closure
of any other stratum
(X_{\mu})^{T},\,\mu\in\Omega
.
On the other hand, the set
{\mathcal{X}}_{\bullet}\cap X^{\prime}_{<\lambda}
is closed
in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\bullet}\cap X^{\prime}
, hence
the set
{\mathcal{X}}_{\bullet}\cap X_{\lambda}=\{x_{\lambda}\}
must be open in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\bullet}\cap X^{\prime}
.
Therefore,
x_{\lambda}
cannot be contained in the closure
of
(X_{\mu})^{T}
for any
\mu\in\Omega\smallsetminus\{\lambda\}
.
We conclude that
\Omega\smallsetminus\{\lambda\}=\emptyset
, as desired.
∎

Corollary 4.1.9
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\lambda}\in D_{T}(X_{\lambda})
, resp.
\mathcal{G}_{<\lambda}\in D_{T}(X^{\prime}_{<\lambda})
,
and let

{\mathbb{H}}_{\lambda}
be either
{\mathbb{H}}(u_{*}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda})

or
{\mathbb{H}}(u_{!}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda})
, resp.
{\mathbb{H}}_{<\lambda}
be
{\mathbb{H}}(v_{*}\mathcal{G}_{<\lambda})
.
Put
{\mathscr{T}}_{<\lambda}=\{\tau\in{\mathscr{T}}\mid{\mathcal{X}}_{\tau}\cap X^{\prime}_{<\lambda}\neq\emptyset\}
. Then, we have:

{{\kern 5.0pt}\sf{(i)}}\;
The idempotent

e_{<\lambda}:=\sum_{\tau\in{\mathscr{T}}_{<\lambda}}e_{\tau}

acts in
Q{\mathbb{H}}_{<\lambda}
, resp.
Q{\mathbb{H}}_{\lambda}
,
as the identity, resp. zero.

{\;\sf{(ii)}}\;
If
{\mathbb{H}}_{<\lambda}
, resp.
{\mathbb{H}}_{\lambda}
, is
{\mathrm{S}}
-torsion free then

\Hom_{A}({\mathbb{H}}_{\lambda},\,{\mathbb{H}}_{<\lambda})=0
, resp.

\Hom_{A}({\mathbb{H}}_{<\lambda},\,{\mathbb{H}}_{\lambda})=0
.

Proof.

The first claim in (i) is clear. To prove the second
claim let
\tau\in{\mathscr{T}}_{<\lambda}
.
Then
{\mathcal{X}}_{\tau}\cap X^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}_{\tau}\cap X^{\prime}_{<\lambda}
, by the previous lemma.
Therefore, the imbedding
\epsilon^{\prime}_{\tau}:{\mathcal{X}}_{\tau}\cap X^{\prime}\,\hookrightarrow\,X^{\prime}

factors through the imbedding
v:X^{\prime}_{<\lambda}\,\hookrightarrow\,X^{\prime}
.
Using that
v^{*}u_{!}=0
, resp.
v^{!}u_{*}=0
, we deduce that
(\epsilon^{\prime}_{\tau})^{*}u_{!}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda}=0
,
resp.
(\epsilon^{\prime}_{\tau})^{!}u_{*}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda}=0
. Hence, from
(
4.1.3
) we obtain

e_{\tau}Q{\mathbb{H}}(u_{!}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda})=e_{\tau}Q{\mathbb{H}}((\epsilon^{\prime}_{\tau})^{*}u_{!}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda})=0,{\kern 5.0pt}\text{resp.}{\kern 5.0pt}e_{\tau}Q{\mathbb{H}}(u_{*}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda})=e_{\tau}Q{\mathbb{H}}((\epsilon^{\prime}_{\tau})^{!}u_{*}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda})=0.

The second
claim in (i) follows.

To prove (ii), let

\phi\in\Hom_{A}({\mathbb{H}}_{\lambda},{\mathbb{H}}_{<\lambda})
. It suffices to show that
the induced
QA
-linear map

Q\phi:Q{\mathbb{H}}_{\lambda}\to Q{\mathbb{H}}_{<\lambda}
equals zero.
For any
m\in Q{\mathbb{H}}_{\lambda}
using (i), we find

Q\phi(m)=e_{<\lambda}\,Q\phi(m)=Q\phi(e_{<\lambda}m)=0
,
as desired.
A similar
argument based on the equation
(1-e_{<\lambda}){\mathbb{H}}_{<\lambda}=0

shows that the image of any
A
-module map

\phi:{\mathbb{H}}_{<\lambda}\to{\mathbb{H}}_{\lambda}
is a torsion
{\mathrm{S}}
-module,
and (ii) follows.
∎

4.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.1.1

Let
X=\sqcup_{s}\,X_{s}
be a decomposition of
X
into connected components
and let
1_{s}\in H^{0}_{T}(X_{s})
be the unit of the algebra
{\mathbb{H}}(X_{s})
.
Then, we have a decomposition
1_{A}=\sum_{s}\,1_{s}
as a sum of orthogonal idempotents.
The action of the idempotents
1_{s}

on the cohomology shows that
for any sheaves
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
supported on different connected components of

X
we have
\Hom^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{H^{0}_{T}(X)}({\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G}),\,{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime}))=0
.
Also, it is clear that
\Ext^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{D_{T}(X)}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})=0
.
Thus, we may (and will) assume that
X
is connected.
In the setting of Lemma
4.1.4
, let
Y_{\lambda}=Y\cap X_{\lambda}

be nonempty and consider the following imbeddings

(4.2.1)

\textstyle{\ Y\cap X^{\prime}_{<\lambda}\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathfrak{i}}_{<\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\bar{v}}
\textstyle{\ Y\cap X^{\prime}\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathfrak{i}}_{\leq\lambda}}
\textstyle{\ Y_{\lambda}\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathfrak{i}}_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\bar{u}}
\textstyle{\ X^{\prime}_{<\lambda}\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\textstyle{\ X^{\prime}\ }
\textstyle{\ X_{\lambda}\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}

In the diagram of the following lemma the vertical maps are induced
by the functor
{\mathbb{H}}(\text{-})
and the groups involving
\bar{u}^{*}\bar{\mathcal{F}}
are declared to be zero if the set
Y_{\lambda}
is empty. We
use shorthand notation

\Ext=\Ext^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{D_{T}(X^{\prime})}
, resp.
\Hom=\Hom^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{A}
.

Lemma 4.2.2
.

Let
\bar{\mathcal{F}}\in D_{T}^{\text{mix}}(Y\cap X^{\prime})
be
!
-pure, resp.

{\mathcal{E}}\in D_{T}^{\text{mix}}(X^{\prime})
be
*
-pure and such that the restriction map
{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}})\to{\mathbb{H}}(u^{*}{\mathcal{E}})

is surjective if
Y_{\lambda}
is nonempty. Then, in the above setting
there is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Ext(v^{*}{\mathcal{E}},({\mathfrak{i}}_{<\lambda})_{*}{\bar{v}}^{!}\bar{\mathcal{F}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{h_{1}}
\textstyle{\Ext({\mathcal{E}},({\mathfrak{i}}_{\leq\lambda})_{*}\bar{\mathcal{F}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{h_{2}}
\textstyle{\Ext(u^{*}{\mathcal{E}},({\mathfrak{i}}_{\lambda})_{*}\bar{u}^{*}\bar{\mathcal{F}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{3}}
\textstyle{0\hskip 9.24994pt{\,}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Hom({\mathbb{H}}(v^{*}{\mathcal{E}}),\,{\mathbb{H}}(({\mathfrak{i}}_{<\lambda})_{*}{\bar{v}}^{!}\bar{\mathcal{F}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\Hom({\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}}),\,{\mathbb{H}}(({\mathfrak{i}}_{\leq\lambda})_{*}\bar{\mathcal{F}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\Hom({\mathbb{H}}(u^{*}{\mathcal{E}}),\,{\mathbb{H}}(({\mathfrak{i}}_{\lambda})_{*}\bar{u}^{*}\bar{\mathcal{F}}))}

of morphisms of graded
A
-modules, such that the following holds:

(1)

The top row
is a short exact sequence of pure and free
{\mathrm{S}}
-modules
(possibly zero).

(2)

The map
\alpha
is injective;

(3)

We have
\im\alpha=\Ker\beta
.

Remark 4.2.3
.

Similar statements may be found
in
[
BY
]
,
[
Gi
]
, and
[
CMNO
]
, Sect. 4.7.2,
but the proof below, based on Corollary
4.1.9
,
is quite different from arguments used in
loc cit
.
In particular,
condition S2 from Sect.
1.1

is essential for the proof.

Proof.

To simplify notation, we put
{\mathcal{F}}=({\mathfrak{i}}_{\leq\lambda})_{*}\bar{\mathcal{F}}
.
Thus,
v^{!}{\mathcal{F}}=({\mathfrak{i}}_{<\lambda})_{*}{\bar{v}}^{!}\bar{\mathcal{F}}
by base change. By adjunction, we have
the following two chains of isomorphisms

(4.2.4)

\displaystyle\Ext(v_{*}v^{*}{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})\cong\Ext(v^{*}{\mathcal{E}},v^{!}{\mathcal{F}})

\displaystyle\cong\Ext(v^{*}{\mathcal{E}},({\mathfrak{i}}_{<\lambda})_{*}{\bar{v}}^{!}\bar{\mathcal{F}})

\displaystyle\cong{\mathbb{H}}({\mathbb{D}}({\mathfrak{i}}_{<\lambda}^{*}v^{*}{\mathcal{E}}){\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}\bar{\mathcal{F}})\cong{\mathbb{H}}(({\mathfrak{i}}_{<\lambda}^{!}v^{!}{\mathbb{D}}{\mathcal{E}}){\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}\bar{\mathcal{F}}),{\kern 5.0pt}\ \text{resp.}

\displaystyle\Ext(u^{*}{\mathcal{E}},({\mathfrak{i}}_{\lambda})_{*}\bar{u}^{*}\bar{\mathcal{F}})\cong\Ext({\mathfrak{i}}_{\lambda}^{*}u^{*}{\mathcal{E}},\bar{u}^{*}\bar{\mathcal{F}})

\displaystyle\cong{\mathbb{H}}(({\mathfrak{i}}_{\lambda}^{!}u^{*}{\mathbb{D}}{\mathcal{E}}){\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}\bar{u}^{*}\bar{\mathcal{F}})

\displaystyle\cong{\mathbb{H}}((\bar{u}^{*}{\mathfrak{i}}_{\leq\lambda}^{!}{\mathbb{D}}{\mathcal{E}}){\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}\bar{u}^{*}\bar{\mathcal{F}})\cong{\mathbb{H}}(\bar{u}^{*}(({\mathfrak{i}}_{\leq\lambda}^{!}{\mathbb{D}}{\mathcal{E}}){\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}\bar{\mathcal{F}})).

Since
the sheaves
({\mathfrak{i}}_{<\lambda}^{!}v^{!}{\mathbb{D}}{\mathcal{E}}){\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}\bar{\mathcal{F}}
and

({\mathfrak{i}}_{\leq\lambda}^{!}{\mathbb{D}}{\mathcal{E}}){\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}\bar{\mathcal{F}}
are
!
-pure, we conclude that
the cohomology group on the right of each chain of isomorphisms,
hence all the
\Ext
-groups above, are pure and free over
{\mathrm{S}}
.

To construct the maps
\bar{\alpha}
and
\bar{\beta}

we apply
the functor
\Ext(\text{-},{\mathcal{F}})
to the distinguished triangle

u_{!}u^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}\to v_{*}v^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
. This gives a long exact sequence

(4.2.5)

\ldots\xrightarrow{\ \partial\ }\Ext^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}(v_{*}v^{*}{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})\xrightarrow{\ \tilde{\alpha}\ }\Ext^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})\xrightarrow{\ \tilde{\beta}\ }\Ext^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}(u_{!}u^{*}{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})\xrightarrow{\ \partial\ }\Ext^{{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}+1}(v_{*}v^{*}{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})\xrightarrow{\ \tilde{\alpha}\ }\ldots

The first, resp. third,
\Ext
-group in (
4.2.5
) is one of the

\Ext
-groups that appear in (
4.2.4
).
These groups being pure and free over
{\mathrm{S}}
,
it follows that so is the group
\Ext({\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}})
; furthermore,
the connecting
homomorphisms
\partial
in (
4.2.5
) vanish.
The resulting short exact sequence gives the top row
of the diagram of the lemma, and (1) holds by construction.

We define the map
\alpha
in the second row of the diagram of the lemma
to be the map that sends

f\in\Hom({\mathbb{H}}(v^{*}{\mathcal{E}}),{\mathbb{H}}(({\mathfrak{i}}_{<\lambda})_{*}{\bar{v}}^{!}\bar{\mathcal{F}}))

to the composition

\alpha(f):\ {\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}})\xrightarrow{v^{*}}{\mathbb{H}}(v^{*}{\mathcal{E}})\xrightarrow{f}{\mathbb{H}}(({\mathfrak{i}}_{<\lambda})_{*}{\bar{v}}^{!}\bar{\mathcal{F}})){\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}{\mathbb{H}}(\bar{v}^{!}\bar{\mathcal{F}})\xrightarrow{\bar{v}_{*}}{\mathbb{H}}(\bar{\mathcal{F}}){\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{F}}).

Since
{\mathcal{E}}
is
*
-pure, resp.
\bar{\mathcal{F}}
is
!
-pure,
the map
v^{*}
, resp.
\bar{v}_{*}
, in the composition is surjective, resp. injective, by Lemma

4.1.4
. Hence,
\alpha
is injective and (2) holds.

Next, we construct the map
\beta
. If the set
Y_{\lambda}
is empty
then
\beta
is the zero map.
If
Y_{\lambda}
is nonempty then the map

u^{*}:{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}})\to{\mathbb{H}}(u^{*}{\mathcal{E}})
is surjective by the assumptions of the lemma, hence
this map induces an isomorphism

{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}})/{\mathbb{H}}(v^{!}{\mathcal{E}}){\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}{\mathbb{H}}(u^{*}{\mathcal{E}})
, by Lemma
4.1.4
(ii).
Let

g\in\Hom_{A}({\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}}),\,{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{F}}))
.
The composition

{\mathbb{H}}(v^{!}{\mathcal{E}})\xrightarrow{v_{*}}{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}})\xrightarrow{g}{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{F}})\xrightarrow{u^{*}}{\mathbb{H}}(u^{*}{\mathcal{F}})\cong{\mathbb{H}}(\bar{u}^{*}\bar{\mathcal{F}})

is the zero map by Corollary
4.1.9
.
It follows that
the map
u^{*}\circ g
has a factorization

{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}})\to{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}})/{\mathbb{H}}(v^{!}{\mathcal{E}}){\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}{\mathbb{H}}(u^{*}{\mathcal{E}})\xrightarrow{\beta(g)}{\mathbb{H}}(({\mathfrak{i}}_{\lambda})_{*}\bar{u}^{*}\bar{\mathcal{F}}){\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}{\mathbb{H}}(u^{*}{\mathcal{F}})

for a unique map
\beta(g)
.
The assignment
g\mapsto\beta(g)
provides the desired
map
\beta
.
It is easy to check that the maps thus defined make the diagram of the lemma
commute.

It remains to prove (3). If
Y_{\lambda}=\emptyset

we have
\bar{u}^{*}=0
and the map
\bar{v}_{*}
is clearly an isomorphism, so
the bottom line is exact, proving (3). We now assume
Y_{\lambda}
is nonempty.
Then, the equation
\beta{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}\alpha=0
holds by construction; explicitly,
we have
\beta(\alpha(f))=\bar{u}^{*}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}\bar{v}_{*}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}{\mathfrak{i}}_{\leq\lambda}^{*}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}f{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}v^{*}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}u_{!}=0
,
since
\bar{u}^{*}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}\bar{v}_{*}=0
.
To prove an opposite inclusion
\Ker(\beta)\subseteq\im(\alpha)
,
let
g:{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}})\to{\mathbb{H}}(\bar{\mathcal{F}})

be such that

\beta(({\mathfrak{i}}_{\leq\lambda})_{*}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}g)=0
, that is, such that

\bar{u}^{*}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}g{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}u_{!}=0
.
By
(
4.1.5
), this means that
\im(\bar{u}^{*}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}g)\subseteq\im(\bar{v}_{*})
, so
we have

g{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}u_{!}=\bar{v}_{*}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}g_{1}

for some
A
-module map
g_{1}
in the diagram:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{H}}_{c}(u^{*}{\mathcal{E}})\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{!}}
\scriptstyle{g_{1}}
\textstyle{\ {\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}})\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{v^{*}}
\textstyle{\ {\mathbb{H}}(v^{*}{\mathcal{E}})\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{3}}
\scriptstyle{g_{2}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{H}}(\bar{v}^{!}\bar{\mathcal{F}})\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{v}_{*}}
\textstyle{\ {\mathbb{H}}(\bar{\mathcal{F}})\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{u}^{*}}
\textstyle{\ {\mathbb{H}}(\bar{u}^{*}\bar{\mathcal{F}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

The map
g_{1}
must be zero by Corollary
4.1.9
,
since
{\mathbb{H}}(\bar{v}^{!}\bar{\mathcal{F}})
is a free
{\mathrm{S}}
-module.
We conclude that
g{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}u_{!}=\bar{v}_{*}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}g_{1}=0
,
so the map
g
sends the image of
u_{!}

to zero. By (
4.1.6
), it follows that
g
factors as

g=g_{2}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}v^{*}
.
Applying Corollary
4.1.9
again, we deduce that
\bar{u}^{*}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}g_{2}=0
.
Hence,
g_{2}
factors as
g_{2}=\bar{v}_{*}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}g_{3}
.
We conclude that

g=\bar{v}_{*}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}g_{2}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}v^{*}
. Thus, we obtain

({\mathfrak{i}}_{\leq\lambda})_{*}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}g=v_{*}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}({\mathfrak{i}}_{<\lambda})_{*}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}g_{2}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}v^{*}=\alpha(({\mathfrak{i}}_{<\lambda})_{*}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}g_{2})\in\im(\alpha)
,
as desired.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.1.1
.

We induct on the number of strata in
X
.
If this number is 1 then
X={\operatorname{pt}}
and the statement is clear.
If
X
has more than one stratum, choose
a subset
X^{\prime}=X_{<\lambda}\sqcup X_{\lambda}\subseteq X
as above, let
i:X^{\prime}\,\hookrightarrow\,X
, resp.
\bar{i}:Y\cap X^{\prime}\,\hookrightarrow\,Y
, denote
the closed imbedding and put
{\mathcal{E}}:=i^{*}{\mathcal{E}}_{X}
, resp.
\bar{\mathcal{F}}:=\bar{i}^{!}{\mathcal{F}}_{Y}
.
Then, we are in the setting of Lemma
4.2.2
, since
the restriction map
u^{*}:{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}})\to{\mathbb{H}}(u^{*}{\mathcal{E}})

is surjective by Lemma
4.1.4
(ii).
By the induction hypothesis, we may assume
that the map
h_{1}
in the diagram of Lemma
4.2.2
is an isomorphism.
If the set
Y\cap X_{\lambda}
is empty then the imbedding
\bar{v}
in the diagram
of the lemma is a bijection and the term in each row that involves
\bar{u}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}

vanishes. It follows that
the
maps
\alpha
and
\bar{\alpha}
are isomorphisms, so the map
h_{2}

is an isomorphism.
In the case where
Y\cap X_{\lambda}
is nonempty the map
h_{3}

in the diagram is an isomorphism since the sheaves

u^{*}{\mathcal{E}}
and
({\mathfrak{i}}_{\lambda})_{*}\bar{u}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}
are direct sums of
shifts of constant sheaves.
Using Lemma
4.2.2
, we deduce
by diagram chase that the map
h_{2}
is an isomorphism,
completing the induction step.
∎

4.3.
!
-tensor product

Let
A
be an arbitrary commutative
{\mathrm{S}}
-algebra. For an
A
-module

M
we let
M^{\vee}:=\Hom_{\mathrm{S}}(M,{\mathrm{S}})
and equip this
{\mathrm{S}}
-module
with an
A
-module structure by
a(f):m\mapsto f(am)
, where
f\in M^{\vee},\,a\in A,\,m\in M
.
For
A
-modules
M,N
, the tensor product

M\otimes_{\mathrm{S}}N
has the natural structure of an
A\otimes_{\mathrm{S}}A
-module, and
we define an
A
-module as follows

(4.3.1)

M{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{A}N\cong\{u\in M\otimes_{\mathrm{S}}N\mid(a\otimes 1)u=(1\otimes a)u\;\ \forall a\in A\}.

In the rest of this section we assume
that
A=\oplus_{i\geq 0}\,A_{i}
is a commutative nonnegatively graded

{\mathrm{S}}
-algebra which is finitely generated as an
{\mathrm{S}}
-module
and such that
A_{0}=\Bbbk
.
Let
A\grfmod
be the category of
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded

A
-modules which are finitely generated and free
as
{\mathrm{S}}
-modules.
A finitely generated graded
A
-module is an object of
A\grfmod
if and only if it is
a maximal Cohen-Macaulay
A
-module.
In particular,
A
itself is a Cohen-Macaulay algebra.
On
A\grfmod
one has an exact contravariant duality

M\mapsto M^{\vee}:=\Hom_{\mathrm{S}}(M,{\mathrm{S}})
such that the canonical map

M\to(M^{\vee})^{\vee}
is an isomorphism.
The
A
-module
A^{\vee}
is the relative dualizing module.
It follows that for any
M\in A\grfmod

there is a canonical isomorphism
M^{\vee}\cong\Hom_{A}(M,A^{\vee})
.

Lemma 4.3.2
.

For
A
as above, the operation
\text{-}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{A}\text{-}
gives
A\grfmod

a symmetric monoidal structure
and for
M,N\in A\grfmod
,
there are canonical isomorphisms of
A
-modules:

(4.3.3)

\displaystyle M{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{A}N\cong(M^{\vee}\otimes_{A}N^{\vee})^{\vee}

(4.3.4)

\displaystyle\Hom_{A}(M,N)\cong M^{\vee}\,{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{A}\,N.

Proof.

For any
M,N\in A\grfmod
one has a canonical isomorphism

M^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathrm{S}}N{\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}\Hom_{\mathrm{S}}(M,N)
of
A\otimes_{\mathrm{S}}A
-modules where
the action of
a\otimes b\in A\otimes_{\mathrm{S}}A
on
f\in\Hom_{\mathrm{S}}(M,N)
is defined by

(a\otimes b)f:m\mapsto af(bm)
.
From this isomorphism
we obtain canonical isomorphisms

\Hom_{A}(M,N)\cong\{f\in\Hom_{\mathrm{S}}(M,N)\mid(a\otimes 1)f=(1\otimes a)f\;\ \forall a\in A\}\cong M^{\vee}\,{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{A}\,N.

This proves (
4.3.4
). Replacing
N
by
N^{\vee}
in the above, we obtain

\displaystyle M^{\vee}\,{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{A}\,N^{\vee}

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{A}(M,N^{\vee})\cong\Hom_{A}(M,\,\Hom_{A}(N,A^{\vee}))

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{A}(M\otimes_{A}N,A^{\vee})\cong(M\otimes_{A}N)^{\vee}.

Replacing
M
by
M^{\vee}
, resp.
N
by
N^{\vee}
, yields (
4.3.3
).
∎

Remark 4.3.5
.

{{\kern 5.0pt}\sf{(i)}}\;
There is a canonical composite
morphism
M{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{A}N\to M\otimes_{\mathrm{S}}N\to M\otimes_{A}N
; the composite is
an isomorphism provided one of the modules is free over
A
.

{\;\sf{(ii)}}\;
Let
I\subseteq A
be the kernel of an
{\mathrm{S}}
-algebra homomorphism

A\to{\mathrm{S}}
.
Then, there is an
{\mathrm{S}}
-stable direct sum decomposition

A\cong{\mathrm{S}}\!\cdot\!1\oplus I
, and for any
A
-module
M

one has

\Hom_{A}(A/I,M)\cong M{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{A}(A/I)\cong M^{I}:=\{m\in M\mid am=0\;\ \forall a\in I\}.

{\sf{(iii)}}\;
Let
A
be a flat commutative
{\mathrm{S}}
-algebra of finite relative injective dimension
and
A^{\vee}=\RHom_{\mathrm{S}}(A,{\mathrm{S}})\in D^{b}(A\mmod)
the relative dualizing complex.
Then, there is a duality
M\mapsto M^{\vee}:=\RHom_{A}(M,A^{\vee})\cong\RHom_{\mathrm{S}}(M,{\mathrm{S}})

on
D^{b}(A\mmod)
and
one may use the RHS of (
4.3.1
) to define a symmetric monoidal
structure on
D^{b}(A\mmod)
.

Let
X=\sqcup_{\lambda}\,X_{\lambda}
be as in Sect.
1.1
and let
{\mathbb{D}}(\text{-})

denote the Verdier duality functor on
D_{T}^{\text{mix}}(X)
.
Recall that the constant sheaf
{\Bbbk}_{X}
, resp. the
dualizing complex
\omega_{X}={\mathbb{D}}{\Bbbk}_{X}
, is
*
-pure, resp.
!
-pure,
and

H^{{\textrm{\tiny{BM}}}}_{T}(X):={\mathbb{H}}(\omega_{X})
is the equivariant Borel-Moore homology.

Part (i) of the following result has been observed earlier by Brion,
[
Br
]
.

Proposition 4.3.6
.

{{\kern 5.0pt}\sf{(i)}}\;
The
{\mathrm{S}}
-algebra
{\mathbb{H}}(X)
is Cohen-Macaulay
and there is a canonical isomorphism

H^{{\textrm{\tiny{BM}}}}_{T}(X)\cong{\mathbb{H}}(X)^{\vee}
.

{\;\sf{(ii)}}\;
If
\mathcal{G}\in D_{T}^{\text{mix}}(X)
is either
!
-pure or
*
-pure then

{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G})\in{\mathbb{H}}(X)\grfmod
and
Verdier duality yields a canonical isomorphism

{\mathbb{H}}({\mathbb{D}}\mathcal{G})\cong({\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G}))^{\vee}
of graded
{\mathbb{H}}(X)
-modules.∎

Proof.

Part (i) follows from (ii).
To prove (ii) let
p_{n}:X_{n}\to B_{n},\,n\geq 1
, be a sequence of finite
dimensional approximations of the Borel construction

X\times_{T}ET\to BT
, e.g. for
T={{\mathbb{G}}_{m}}
one can take
B_{n}={\mathbb{P}}^{n}
. Thus,

BT
is a direct limit of
a chain
\ldots\xrightarrow{i_{n}}B_{n}\xrightarrow{i_{n+1}}B_{n+1}\xrightarrow{i_{n+2}}\ldots
,
of closed imbeddings and the maps
p_{n}
are smooth projective morphisms with fiber
X
,
so
(p_{n})_{*}=(p_{n})_{!}
.
Let

\mathcal{G}_{n}
, resp.
({\mathbb{D}}\mathcal{G})_{n}
, be either
!
-pure or
*
-pure sheaf on
X_{n}
that corresponds to

\mathcal{G}
, resp.
{\mathbb{D}}\mathcal{G}
. The collection of sheaves
\mathcal{G}_{n},\,n\geq 1
, comes
equipped with isomorphisms

(p_{n})_{*}\mathcal{G}_{n}\cong i_{n+1}^{*}(p_{n+1})_{*}\mathcal{G}_{n+1}
,
and
the inverse system
\ldots\leftarrow H^{k}((p_{n})_{*}\mathcal{G}_{n})\leftarrow H^{k}((p_{n+1})_{*}\mathcal{G}_{n+1})\leftarrow\ldots
, stabilizes for each integer
k
.
The stable limit is, by definition,
the equivariant cohomology group
{\mathbb{H}}^{k}(\mathcal{G})
.
We know by Corollary
4.1.7
applied in the case of a trivial torus
that the nonequivariant cohomology of
\mathcal{G}
and
{\mathbb{D}}\mathcal{G}

are both pure.
Since
B_{n}
is simply connected, it follows from the Leray spectral sequence that
for every
n
the
objects
(p_{n})_{*}\mathcal{G}_{n},\,(p_{n})_{*}({\mathbb{D}}\mathcal{G})_{n}\in D^{mix}(B_{n})
are pure, hence quasi-isomorphic
to a direct sum of shifts of the constant sheaf
{\Bbbk}_{B_{n}}
.
We have
(p_{n})_{*}({\mathbb{D}}\mathcal{G})_{n}\cong{\mathbb{D}}((p_{n})_{*}\mathcal{G}_{n})

since
p_{n}
is proper. Thus, Verdier duality
yields

H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}((p_{n})_{*}({\mathbb{D}}\mathcal{G}_{n}))\,\cong\,H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}({\mathbb{D}}((p_{n})_{*}\mathcal{G}_{n}))\,\cong\,\Hom^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}(B_{n})}(H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}((p_{n})_{*}\mathcal{G}_{n}),\,H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}(B_{n})).

The desired isomorphism
{\mathbb{H}}({\mathbb{D}}\mathcal{G})\cong({\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G}))^{\vee}
follows from this
by taking an inverse limit.
∎

Corollary 4.3.7
.

For any
{\mathcal{F}}\in D_{T}(Y)
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{E}}_{n}\in D_{T}(X)
,
there is a natural morphism

{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{F}}\,{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}\,{\mathfrak{i}}^{!}({\mathcal{E}}_{1}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}\ldots{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}{\mathcal{E}}_{n}))\to{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{F}}){\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{{\mathbb{H}}(X)}{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}}_{1}){\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{{\mathbb{H}}(X)}\ldots{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{{\mathbb{H}}(X)}{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}}_{n}).

If
{\mathcal{F}}
is
!
-pure and all
{\mathcal{E}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{E}}_{n}
are pure then the above morphism
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{G}={\mathcal{F}}\boxtimes{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\boxtimes\ldots\boxtimes{\mathcal{E}}_{n}

and
i:Y\,\hookrightarrow\,Y\times X^{n},\,y\mapsto(y,{\mathfrak{i}}(y),\ldots,{\mathfrak{i}}(y))
, a closed imbeddng.
Thus, we have
{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{F}}\,{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}\,{\mathfrak{i}}^{!}({\mathcal{E}}_{1}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}\ldots{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}{\mathcal{E}}_{n}))={\mathbb{H}}(i^{!}\mathcal{G})
.
The image of the adjunction

{\mathbb{H}}(i_{!}i^{!}\mathcal{G})\to{\mathbb{H}}(\mathcal{G})

is annihilated by the action of the kernel
\Ker\subset{\mathbb{H}}(Y\times X^{n})
of the restriction
map
i^{*}:{\mathbb{H}}(Y\times X^{n})\to{\mathbb{H}}(Y)
. Therefore, this image is contained
in a subspace of
{\mathbb{H}}(Y)
formed by the elements annihilated by
\Ker
.
This subspace is

{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{F}}){\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{{\mathbb{H}}(X)}{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}}_{1}){\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{{\mathbb{H}}(X)}\ldots{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{{\mathbb{H}}(X)}{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}}_{n})
,
by the definition of
{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}
.

Assume next that
{\mathcal{F}}
is
!
-pure, resp.
{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{E}}_{1}
is pure, and
n=1
.
Then, the morphism in the statement may be factored as
the following composition:

{\mathbb{H}}^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}(({\mathfrak{i}}^{!}{\mathcal{E}}){\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}{\mathcal{F}}){\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}{\mathbb{H}}^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}({\mathbb{D}}({\mathbb{D}}{\mathcal{E}})\,{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}\,{\mathfrak{i}}_{*}{\mathcal{F}}){\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}\Ext^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{D(X)}({\mathbb{D}}{\mathcal{E}},\,{\mathfrak{i}}_{*}{\mathcal{F}})\\
{\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}\Hom^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{{\mathbb{H}}(X)}({\mathbb{H}}({\mathbb{D}}{\mathcal{E}}),\,{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{F}})){\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}{\mathbb{H}}({\mathbb{D}}{\mathcal{E}})^{\vee}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{F}}){\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}}){\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{{\mathbb{H}}(X)}{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{F}}).

Here, the third, resp. fourth and fifth, isomorphism holds
by Theorem
1.1.1
, resp. part (ii) of
Proposition
4.3.6
, and (
4.3.4
).
The general case of an arbitrary
n\geq 1
may be reduced
to the case
n=2
by induction on
n
using the associativity of the
monoidal structure
{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{{\mathbb{H}}(X)}
on the category
{\mathbb{H}}(X)\grfmod
.
∎

Corollary 4.3.8
.

In the setting of Sect.
1.1
, let
\Theta\subseteq\Sigma
be a subset such that

Y_{\Theta}=\sqcup_{\nu\in\Theta}\,Y_{\nu}
is a locally closed
subvariety of
X
and
{\mathfrak{i}}_{\Theta}:Y_{\Theta}\,\hookrightarrow\,X
,
resp.
\jmath_{\mu}:X_{\mu}\,\hookrightarrow\,X
, the imbedding.
Then, for a pure
{\mathcal{E}}\in D_{T}^{\text{mix}}(X)
the adjunction
({\mathfrak{i}}_{\Theta})_{!}{\mathfrak{i}}_{\Theta}^{!}\to{\operatorname{Id}}
,
resp.
(\jmath_{\mu})_{!}\jmath_{\mu}^{!}\to{\operatorname{Id}}
,
induces an isomorphism

(4.3.9)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{H}}_{c}(Y_{\Theta},{\mathfrak{i}}_{\Theta}^{!}{\mathcal{E}})\

\displaystyle{\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}\ \Hom^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{{\mathbb{H}}(X)}({\mathbb{H}}_{c}(Y_{\Theta}),\,{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}})),{\kern 5.0pt}\text{resp.}

(4.3.10)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{H}}(\jmath_{\mu}^{!}{\mathcal{E}})\

\displaystyle{\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}\Hom^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{{\mathbb{H}}(X)}({\mathbb{H}}(X_{\mu}),\,{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{E}})),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mu\in\Sigma.

Proof.

We apply Corollary
4.3.7
in the case
n=1
.
Taking
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathfrak{i}}_{*}\omega_{Y}\in D_{T}^{\text{mix}}(Y)
yields (
4.3.9
).
Similarly, to prove (
4.3.10
) one takes
Y=Y_{\Theta}=X_{\mu}
and

{\mathcal{F}}=(\jmath_{\mu})_{*}{\Bbbk}_{X_{\mu}}
.
The desired isomorphism then follows using that

{\mathbb{H}}({\mathfrak{i}}_{!}{\mathfrak{i}}^{!}{\mathcal{E}})\cong{\mathbb{H}}_{c}({\mathfrak{i}}^{!}{\mathcal{E}})\cong{\mathbb{H}}({\mathfrak{i}}^{!}{\mathcal{E}})[-2\dim X_{\mu}]
, resp.

{\mathbb{H}}_{c}(X_{\mu})\cong{\mathbb{H}}(X_{\mu})[-2\dim X_{\mu}]
.
∎

5.
Reminder on derived Satake

5.1.
Generalities

Fix a maximal torus
T\subseteq G
and an Iwahori subgroup

I\subseteq G(O)
that contains
T
.

The affine Grassmannian
{\mathrm{Gr}}={\mathrm{Gr}}_{G}
can be presented as a direct limit

{\mathrm{Gr}}=\underset{\mathrel{{\mathop{{n}}\limits}}}{\lim}\,X_{n}
of
G(O)
-stable projective subvarieties

X_{n}\subseteq{\mathrm{Gr}}
.
For each
{n}
, the
I
-orbits
X_{\lambda}=I.\lambda,\,\lambda\in{\mathbb{X}}_{*}(T)
, contained in
X_{n}
form a
T
-stable stratification

X_{n}=\sqcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}}X_{\lambda}
,
where
\Lambda_{n}\subseteq{\mathbb{X}}_{*}(T)
is a finite subset.
Let
i_{n}:X_{n}\,\hookrightarrow\,X_{{n}+1}
denote the closed imbedding.
Thus, one has the category
D_{I}({\mathrm{Gr}})=\underset{\,\longrightarrow\,}{\lim}\,D_{I}(X_{n})

and
\underset{\longleftarrow}{\lim}\,\Ind D_{I}(X_{n})
, an inverse limit of the categories

D_{I}(X_{n})
along
!
-restrictions. An object
of
\Ind D_{I}({\mathrm{Gr}})

is an inverse limit
{\mathcal{F}}=\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,{\mathcal{F}}_{n}

of objects

{\mathcal{F}}_{n}\in D_{I}(X_{n})
equipped with isomorphisms

\kappa_{n}:i_{n}^{!}{\mathcal{F}}_{{n}+1}{\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
which satisfy natural compatibilities,
cf. eg.
[
BFN2
, Sect. 2(i)]
.
In particular, one has the dualizing sheaf
\omega_{\mathrm{Gr}}=\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,\omega_{X_{n}}\in\Ind D_{I}({\mathrm{Gr}})
.
There is also the
G(O)
-equivariant counterpart

\Ind{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}
defined in a similar fashion.

Let
{\mathfrak{t}}=\Lie T
and
{\mathbb{H}}(\text{-})=H_{T}^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}(\text{-})\cong H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{I}(\text{-})
, so

{\mathbb{H}}({\operatorname{pt}})\cong\Bbbk[{\mathfrak{t}}]
, where
{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}
is placed in degree
2
.
The constant sheaf
{\Bbbk}_{\mathrm{Gr}}
is not an object of
\Ind D_{I}({\mathrm{Gr}})

since it is not an inverse limit along
!
-restrictions; instead,
one has natural isomorphisms
i^{*}_{n}{\Bbbk}_{X_{{n}+1}}{\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}{\Bbbk}_{X_{{n}}}
.
The compatible cell decompositions of the
X_{n}
’s insure
that the restriction maps
i^{*}_{n}:{\mathbb{H}}(X_{{n}+1})\to{\mathbb{H}}(X_{n})
are surjective;
furtheremore, for each
m
, the inverse system of cohomology

\ldots\leftarrow{\mathbb{H}}^{m}(X_{n})\leftarrow{\mathbb{H}}^{m}(X_{n+1})\leftarrow\ldots
stabilizes. Let

{\mathbb{H}}^{m}({\mathrm{Gr}})
be this stable limit and

{\mathbb{H}}({\mathrm{Gr}}):=\oplus_{m}\,{\mathbb{H}}^{m}({\mathrm{Gr}})
. Then, for any
{n}
the restriction
map
{\mathbb{H}}({\mathrm{Gr}})\to{\mathbb{H}}(X_{n})
is a surjective map of graded
\Bbbk[{\mathfrak{t}}]
-algebras, so
we may (and will) view
{\mathbb{H}}(X_{n})
-modules
as
{\mathbb{H}}({\mathrm{Gr}})
-modules.
For
{\mathcal{F}}=\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,{\mathcal{F}}_{n}\in\Ind D_{I}({\mathrm{Gr}})
,
one has an

{\mathbb{H}}(X_{n})
-module structure on
{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{n})
.
The maps
{\mathbb{H}}^{m}({\mathcal{F}}_{k})\xleftarrow[\cong]{\kappa_{k}}{\mathbb{H}}^{m}(i_{k}^{!}{\mathcal{F}}_{k+1})\xrightarrow{(i_{k})_{!}}{\mathbb{H}}^{m}({\mathcal{F}}_{k+1})
are
{\mathbb{H}}(X_{n})
-module maps for all
k<{n}
.
This way, the groups
{\mathbb{H}}^{m}({\mathcal{F}}_{n}),\,{n}\geq 1
, form a direct system
and we define
{\mathbb{H}}^{m}({\mathcal{F}}):=\underset{\rightarrow}{\lim}\,{\mathbb{H}}^{m}({\mathcal{F}}_{n})
.
The above shows that the total cohomology
{\mathbb{H}}({\mathcal{F}})=\oplus_{m}\,{\mathbb{H}}^{m}({\mathcal{F}})

acquires the structure of a graded
{\mathbb{H}}({\mathrm{Gr}})
-module.

In the case of a finite ground field
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
and a split reductive group

G
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, one has the affine Grassmannian over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
,
which is a direct limit of projective subvarieties
X_{n}
, as above, defined over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
.
Let
{\mathrm{Gr}}
be a
base change to
{\mathbb{F}}=\overline{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
and

D_{I}({\mathrm{Gr}})
, resp.
{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}
, the corresponding category of
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-adic sheaves.
This category is equipped with a pullback functor
Fr^{*}

along the geometric Frobenius.
An object
{\mathcal{F}}\in\Ind D_{I}({\mathrm{Gr}})
is called
!
-pure if
{\mathcal{F}}=\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,{\mathcal{F}}_{n}

where each
{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
has a lift to a
!
-pure object
of
D^{mix}_{I}(X_{n})
.
On defines pure objects in a similar fashion.

5.2.

We choose a maximal compact subgroup
G_{c}\subset G
.
We write
{\mathsf{H}}(\text{-}):=H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{G(O)}(\text{-})
and let
W=N(T)/T
denote the Weyl group.
For
{\mathcal{F}}\in D_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})
, there are canonical
isomorphisms

(5.2.1)

{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}})\cong H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{G_{c}}(\text{Obl}^{G(O)}_{G_{c}}{\mathcal{F}}),\hskip 10.00002ptH^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{T}(\text{Obl}^{G(O)}_{T}{\mathcal{F}})\cong\Bbbk[{\mathfrak{t}}]\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}),\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}})\cong\big(H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{T}(\text{Obl}^{G(O)}_{T}{\mathcal{F}})\big)^{W}.

Let
{\mathsf{C}}:=\Bbbk[\mathfrak{g}]^{G}\cong\Bbbk[{\mathfrak{t}}]^{W}
, resp.
{\mathfrak{c}}={\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}=\Spec{\mathsf{C}}\cong{\mathfrak{t}}/W\cong{\check{\mathfrak{g}}}^{*}/\!/{\check{G}}
.
We have
{\mathsf{C}}\cong{\mathsf{H}}({\operatorname{pt}})\cong H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{G_{c}}({\operatorname{pt}})
. This allows
to transfer various results for

T
-equivariant cohomology to results for
G(O)
-equivariant cohomology.

Let
\Omega_{pol}G_{c}\subset G(K)
be the subgroup of based polynomial maps

S^{1}\to G_{c}
. The algebra
H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{G_{c}}(\Omega G_{c})

has the structure of a commutative and cocommutative
graded Hopf algebra over
{\mathsf{C}}
with coproduct

\Delta:H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{G_{c}}(\Omega G_{c})\to H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{G_{c}}(\Omega G_{c})\,\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}\,H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{G_{c}}(\Omega G_{c})

induced
by pointwise multiplication
\Omega G_{c}\times\Omega G_{c}\to\Omega G_{c}
.
Recall that the cotangent sheaf

T^{*}_{{\mathfrak{c}}}
is a free
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathfrak{c}}}
-module with basis
df,\,f\in\Bbbk[{\mathfrak{c}}]
,
and there is an isomorphism
\Lie J\cong T^{*}_{{\mathfrak{c}}}
.
Let
{\mathfrak{j}}={\mathfrak{j}}_{\check{G}}:=\Gamma({\mathfrak{c}},\Lie J)\cong\Gamma({\mathfrak{c}},T^{*}_{\mathfrak{c}})
.
Thus,
{\mathfrak{j}}
is a free
{\mathsf{C}}
-module of rank
\dim{\mathfrak{t}}
.
It is known that there is a Hopf algebra isomorphism,
[
BF
]
,
[
BFM
]
, and
[
YZ
]
.

(5.2.2)

H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{G_{c}}(\Omega G_{c})\cong\sym_{\mathsf{C}}{\mathfrak{j}}

such that
the space of primitive elements
of the Hopf algebra
H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{G_{c}}(\Omega G_{c})
corresponds to
{\mathfrak{j}}
.
Further, it is known that the composite
\Omega_{pol}G_{c}\,\hookrightarrow\,G(K)\,\twoheadrightarrow\,{\mathrm{Gr}}

is a homeomorphism,
cf.
[
Nad
, Sect. 4]
.
Hence, we obtain

{\mathsf{H}}({\mathrm{Gr}})\cong H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{G_{c}}({\mathrm{Gr}})\cong H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{G_{c}}(\Omega G_{c})
, so
the Hopf algebra structure on
H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{G_{c}}(\Omega G_{c})\cong\sym_{\mathsf{C}}{\mathfrak{j}}

may be transported to
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathrm{Gr}})
via the isomorphisms.
Dually, there is a commutative and cocomutative Hopf
algebra structure on
H^{\textrm{\tiny{BM}}}_{G_{c}}(\Omega G_{c})
which can be transported to

H^{\textrm{\tiny{BM}}}_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})
.
Thus,
\Spec H^{\textrm{\tiny{BM}}}_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})
acquires the structure
of a commutative group scheme over
{\mathfrak{c}}
.

Let
\star
be the convolution monoidal structure on
{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}
.
For any
{\mathcal{F}}_{1},{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\in{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}
there are
isomorphisms

(5.2.3)

R\Gamma_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}},{\mathcal{F}}_{1})\,{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{\mathsf{C}}\,R\Gamma_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}},{\mathcal{F}}_{2}){\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}R\Gamma_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}},\,{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\boxtimes{\mathcal{F}}_{2}){\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}R\Gamma_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}},{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\star{\mathcal{F}}_{2}),

in
D({\mathsf{H}}\mmod)
,
where the first isomorphism is the derived Kunneth formula.
The composite of these maps, to be also denoted
\star
,
equips the functor

R\Gamma_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}},\text{-}):{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}\to D({\mathsf{C}}\mmod)
with a
monoidal structure. If at least one of the sheaves
{\mathcal{F}}_{i},\,i=1,2
, is
G(O)
-equivariantly formal
then (
5.2.3
)
induces an isomorphism

(5.2.4)

\star:\,{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{1})\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{2}){\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{1}\star{\mathcal{F}}_{2}).

The
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathrm{Gr}})
-action on cohomology
is compatible with the coproduct on
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathrm{Gr}})
in the sense
that for all
h\in{\mathsf{H}}({\mathrm{Gr}})
the following diagram
commutes

(5.2.5)

\textstyle{{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{1})\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\star}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\Delta(h)}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{1}\star{\mathcal{F}}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{1})\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\star}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{1}\star{\mathcal{F}}_{2})}

The natural action of
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathrm{Gr}})
on
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}})

gives,
thanks to the isomorphism
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathrm{Gr}})\cong\sym_{\mathsf{C}}{\mathfrak{j}}
,
a
{\mathfrak{j}}
-module structure on
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}})
.
It was shown by Yun and Zhu,
[
YZ
, Lemma 3.1]
, that the
{\mathfrak{j}}
-action on
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}})

can be exponentiated to a
J
-action. Specifically, write

{\mathcal{F}}=\underset{\longleftarrow}{\lim}\,{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
.
Fix
i
and let
X\subset{\mathrm{Gr}}
be a
G(O)
-stable projective subvariety that contains
\supp{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
.
Since
X
is a union of Iwahori orbits,
H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{G(O)}(X)
is a free
{\mathsf{C}}
-module so

H^{G(O)}_{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}(X)=H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{G(O)}(X)^{\vee}
. Let
\{h^{i}\}

and
\{h_{i}\}
be dual bases of

H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{G(O)}(X)
and
H^{G(O)}_{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}({\mathrm{Gr}})
, respectively, with respect to the
perfect pairing
H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{G(O)}(X)\times H^{G(O)}_{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}({\mathrm{Gr}})\to{\mathsf{C}}
.
Define a map
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{i})\to H_{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}^{G(O)}(X)\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{i})

by the formula

m\mapsto\sum_{i}\,h_{i}\otimes(h^{i}m)
. This map is independent of the choice of bases and
it was shown in
[
YZ
]
that the composite

{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{i})\to H_{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}^{G(O)}(X)\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{i})\to H^{{\textrm{\tiny{BM}}}}_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{i})

gives a coaction of the coalgebra
{\Bbbk}[J]
on
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{i})
.
Furthermore, the differential of the resulting
J
-action agrees with the
{\mathfrak{j}}
-action
considered earlier.

Any pure object of
{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}
is (up to Tate twists of its mixed lift)
isomorphic to a finite direct sum of objects of the form
{\operatorname{IC}}({\overline{{\mathrm{Gr}}_{{\lambda}}}})[m]

for some
\lambda\in{\mathbb{X}}_{+}
and
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
We say that
{\mathcal{E}}\in\Ind{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}
is pure if it is isomorphic to
a countable direct sum

\oplus_{i}\,{\operatorname{IC}}({\overline{{\mathrm{Gr}}_{{\lambda_{i}}}}})[m_{i}]
where
\lambda_{i}\in{\mathbb{X}}_{+}
and
m_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Recall the lax monoidal functor
\text{-}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}\text{-}
on
{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}
.
For ind-objects
{\mathcal{F}}=\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,{\mathcal{F}}_{n}
and

\mathcal{G}=\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,\mathcal{G}_{n}
, there is a well defined object

{\mathcal{F}}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}\mathcal{G}=\underset{\leftarrow}{\lim}\,{\mathcal{F}}_{n}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}\mathcal{G}_{n}\in\Ind D_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})
.

Lemma 5.2.6
.

If
{\mathcal{F}}\in\Ind{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}
is
!
-pure then
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}})
is pure
and we have:

{{\kern 5.0pt}\sf{(i)}}\;
The cohomology
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}})
is a direct limit of a sequence
of finite rank free
{\mathsf{C}}
-submodules of
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}})
;
in particular, the
{\mathsf{C}}
-submodule
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}})
is faithfully flat.

{\;\sf{(ii)}}\;
If
{\mathcal{E}}\in\Ind{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}
is pure then there is
an isomorphism

{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{E}}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}{\mathcal{F}}){\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{E}})\otimes_{{\mathsf{C}}}^{J}{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}})
.
Moreover, if
{\mathcal{E}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}
are commutative ring objects
then this is a ring isomorphism.

Proof.

Part (i) follows from Corollary
4.1.7
by taking
W
-invariants,
cf. (
5.2.1
).
The proof of (ii) is based on the observation that for any

M\in\qcoh^{J}({\mathfrak{c}})
which is flat over
{\mathfrak{c}}

the natural inclusion

\Gamma({\mathfrak{c}},M^{J})\subseteq\Gamma({\mathfrak{c}},M)^{{\mathfrak{j}}}
is an equality.
To prove this, one uses that the fibers of the restriction
of the group scheme
J\to{\mathfrak{c}}
to a Zariski open dense subset

U\subseteq{\mathfrak{c}}
are tori. This implies that
for any section
m\in\Gamma({\mathfrak{c}},M)^{{\mathfrak{j}}}
, the
restriction of
m
to
U
is fixed by the group scheme
J|_{U}
,
since tori are connected.
The sheaf
M
being flat, it follows that
m
is itself fixed by
J
,
as desired.

Now, let
{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}}
be as in the statement.
Thus,
{\mathcal{E}}=\oplus_{i}\,,{\operatorname{IC}}({\overline{{\mathrm{Gr}}_{{\lambda_{i}}}}})[m_{i}]
,
resp.
{\mathcal{F}}=\underset{\mathrel{{\mathop{i}\limits}}}{\lim}\,{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
,
where
X_{i}\subseteq{\mathrm{Gr}}
is a
G(O)
-stable projective variety that contains

{\overline{{\mathrm{Gr}}_{{\lambda_{k}}}}}
for all
k\leq i
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
is a
!
-pure sheaf supported on
X_{i}
.
By Sect.
5.1
we have

{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}})=\underset{\to}{\lim}\,{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{i})
where
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{i})
are finite rank free
{\mathsf{C}}
-modules.
We apply Corollary
4.3.7
for
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{F}}_{i}
, resp.
{\mathcal{E}}={\operatorname{IC}}({\overline{{\mathrm{Gr}}_{{\lambda_{i}}}}})
.
Since the algebra
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathrm{Gr}})\cong\sym_{\mathsf{C}}{\mathfrak{j}}
is generated by
{\mathfrak{j}}
,
using (
5.2.1
) and taking
W
-invariants of
T
-equivariant cohomology we deduce

\displaystyle{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{E}}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}{\mathcal{F}})=\underset{\mathrel{{\mathop{i}\limits}}}{\lim}\,\Big(\bigoplus_{k\leq i}\,

\displaystyle{\mathsf{H}}({\operatorname{IC}}({\overline{{\mathrm{Gr}}_{{\lambda_{k}}}}})[m_{k}]{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}{\mathcal{F}}_{i})\Big)\cong\underset{\mathrel{{\mathop{i}\limits}}}{\lim}\,\Big(\bigoplus_{k\leq i}\,{\mathsf{H}}({\overline{{\mathrm{Gr}}_{{\lambda_{k}}}}})[m_{k}]{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{{\mathsf{H}}(X_{i})}{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{i})\Big)

\displaystyle\cong{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{E}})\,{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{{\mathsf{H}}({\mathrm{Gr}})}\,{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}})\cong{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{E}})\,\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}^{\mathfrak{j}}\,{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}})\cong{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{E}})\,\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}^{J}\,{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}),

where the last isomorphism follows from the first paragraph of the proof
since
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{E}})
and
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}})
are flat
{\mathsf{C}}
-modules.
The statement about ring structures is a direct consequence
of the lax monoidality of the functors
\text{-}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}\text{-}
and
{\mathsf{H}}
.
∎

5.3.
Geometric Satake equivalence and the functor
\Phi

Recall that
{\mathbb{X}}_{+}\subseteq{\mathbb{X}}^{*}({\check{T}})
denotes the dominant Weyl chamber.
The abelian Satake category
{{\mathcal{P}}erv}_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})

is known to be a semisimple category with simple objects

{\operatorname{IC}}_{\lambda}={\operatorname{IC}}({\overline{{\mathrm{Gr}}_{{\lambda}}}}),\,\lambda\in{\mathbb{X}}_{+}
; furthermore, this category is
stable under convolution,
[
MV
]
.
All objects of

{{\mathcal{P}}erv}_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})
are pure, hence equivariantly formal. It follows that
the lax monoidal functor
{\mathsf{H}}

restricts to a monoidal functor
{\mathsf{H}}:{{\mathcal{P}}erv}_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})\to{\mathsf{C}}\proj
,
where
{\mathsf{C}}\proj
is the category of finitely generated graded projective,
hence free
{\mathsf{C}}
-modules.

We now equip the convolution product
on
{{\mathcal{P}}erv}_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})
with a (super)symmetry constraint,
bypassing the Beilinson-Drinfeld Grassmannian and fusion,

[
MV
]
,
[
BD
]
.
To this end, for any
{\mathcal{F}}_{1},{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\in{{\mathcal{P}}erv}_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})
we consider the following chain
of isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\Hom_{{{\mathcal{P}}erv}_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}({\mathcal{F}}_{1}\!\star\!{\mathcal{F}}_{2},{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\!\star\!{\mathcal{F}}_{1})

\displaystyle{\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}\Ext^{0}_{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}({\mathcal{F}}_{1}\!\star\!{\mathcal{F}}_{2},{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\!\star\!{\mathcal{F}}_{1})

(5.3.1)

\displaystyle{\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}\Hom^{0}_{{\mathsf{H}}({\mathrm{Gr}})}\big({\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{1}\!\star\!{\mathcal{F}}_{2}),{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{2}\!\star\!{\mathcal{F}}_{1})\big)

\displaystyle{\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}\Hom^{0}_{{\mathsf{H}}({\mathrm{Gr}})}\big({\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{1})\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{2}),\,{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{2})\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{1})\big),

where the first isomorphism holds since the category
{{\mathcal{P}}erv}_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})
is the
heart of the perverse t-structure on
{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}

and the second isomorphism holds by Theorem
1.1.1
.
The cocommutativity of the coproduct
\Delta
on the Hopf algebra
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathrm{Gr}})
provides a canonical
isomorphism

\phi_{{\mathcal{F}}_{1},{\mathcal{F}}_{2}}:{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{1})\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{2}){\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{2})\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}{\mathsf{H}}({\mathcal{F}}_{1})

of
{\mathsf{H}}({\mathrm{Gr}})
-modules.
We define the desired super-symmetry constraint

\varphi_{{\mathcal{F}}_{1},{\mathcal{F}}_{2}}:{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\star{\mathcal{F}}_{2}{\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\star{\mathcal{F}}_{1}

to be the preimage of
\phi_{{\mathcal{F}}_{1},{\mathcal{F}}_{2}}

under the composite isomorphism in (
5.3.1
).
The signs appearing in the super-symmetry constraint are removed either as
it has been traditionally done in

[
BD
]
,
[
MV
]
, or using shearing as in
[
BZSV
]
.
All compatibilities required for the resulting symmetry constraint
are immediate consequences of the axioms
of Hopf algebra.

Next, we recall the construction of the monoidal equivalence

\Phi:D_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}}){\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}D^{{\check{G}}}({\sym({\check{\mathfrak{g}}}[-2])})
introduced in

[
BFN2
, Sect. 5]
. This functor is related
to the derived Satake equivalence
\Psi:{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}{\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}D^{{\check{G}}}_{\text{perf}}(S{\check{\mathfrak{g}}})

of Bezrukavnikov and Finkelberg by the formula

\Phi={\mathscr{C}}\textit{h}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}\Psi^{-1}
,
where
{\mathscr{C}}\textit{h}^{*}
is the auto-equivalence induced by a Chevalley
involution
{\mathscr{C}}\textit{h}:{\check{G}}\to{\check{G}}
.
Let
{\boldsymbol{1}}={\operatorname{IC}}({\mathrm{Gr}}_{0})
denote
the skyscraper sheaf at the base point of
{\mathrm{Gr}}
.
We may view
\Bbbk[{\check{G}}]

as a ring object of
\Ind\Rep{\check{G}}
via the
{\check{G}}
-action on itself by

left
translations.
Therefore, the regular sheaf
{\mathcal{R}}:=\phi^{-1}(\Bbbk[{\check{G}}])=\oplus_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{X}}_{+}}\,{\operatorname{IC}}({\overline{{\mathrm{Gr}}_{{\lambda}}}})\otimes V_{\lambda}^{*}
has
the structure of a ring object in
\Ind{{\mathcal{P}}erv}_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})
. Thus,
{\mathcal{R}}
is equipped
with a morphism
m:{\mathcal{R}}\star{\mathcal{R}}\to{\mathcal{R}}

and the unit
{\boldsymbol{1}}\to{\mathcal{R}}
subject to natural compatibilities.
The
{\check{G}}
-action on the
V_{\lambda}^{*}
’s, i.e. the action on
\Bbbk[{\check{G}}]
by
right
translations,
gives an action of
{\check{G}}
on
{\mathcal{R}}
by ring object automorphisms.

Following
[
BFN2
, Sect. 5(vii)]
, we define the functor

\Phi:{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}\to D_{\text{perf}}^{\check{G}}({\mathsf{C}}),{\kern 5.0pt}{\mathcal{F}}\mapsto{\operatorname{R}\!\Gamma_{G(O)}}({\mathrm{Gr}},{\mathcal{R}}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}{\mathcal{F}}).

This functor has a monoidal structure constructed as follows.
First of all, the lax monoidal structure on
{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}
provides,
for each pair
{\mathcal{F}}_{1},{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\in{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}
, a morphism

({\mathcal{R}}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}{\mathcal{F}}_{1})\star({\mathcal{R}}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}{\mathcal{F}}_{2})\to({\mathcal{R}}\star{\mathcal{R}}){\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}({\mathcal{F}}_{1}\star{\mathcal{F}}_{2})\xrightarrow{m}{\mathcal{R}}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}({\mathcal{F}}_{1}\star{\mathcal{F}}_{2}).

Applying
R\Gamma_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}},\text{-})
to the composite
we get, since
R\Gamma_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}},\text{-})
is a monoidal functor,
a lax monoidal structure

\Phi({\mathcal{F}}_{1})\,{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{\mathsf{C}}\,\Phi({\mathcal{F}}_{2})\,\to\,\Phi({\mathcal{F}}_{1}\star{\mathcal{F}}_{2})
on
\Phi
.
It is not difficult to show that the morphism

\Phi({\boldsymbol{1}})\,{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{\mathsf{C}}\,\Phi({\boldsymbol{1}})\,\to\,\Phi({\boldsymbol{1}})

can be
upgraded to a dg algebra structure on
{\mathsf{R}}:=\Phi({\boldsymbol{1}})
,
cf. Sect.
6.1
. Similarly,
the morphisms
\Phi({\boldsymbol{1}})\,{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}_{\mathsf{C}}\,\Phi({\mathcal{F}})\,\to\,\Phi({\mathcal{F}})
provide an
upgrading of
\Phi
to a functor

\Phi:{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}\to D_{\text{perf}}^{\check{G}}({\mathsf{R}})
.

Let
S{\check{\mathfrak{g}}}={\sym({\check{\mathfrak{g}}}[-2])}
.
Next, mimicing arguments in
[
BF
, Sect. 6.5]
based on purity,
one shows that the dg algebra

{\mathsf{R}}

is formal, i.e. quasi-isomorphic to the graded
{\mathsf{C}}
-algebra
H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}({\mathsf{R}})
,
cf. Sect.
The derived Satake equivalence
\Psi:{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}{\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}D^{{\check{G}}}_{perf}(S{\check{\mathfrak{g}}})
of
[
BF
]
sends

{\boldsymbol{1}}
to
S{\check{\mathfrak{g}}}
, resp.
{\mathcal{R}}
to
S{\check{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes\Bbbk[{\check{G}}]
.
We deduce

\displaystyle H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}({\mathsf{R}})\cong{\mathsf{H}}({\boldsymbol{1}}{\mathrel{{\mathop{\otimes}\limits}}}{\mathcal{R}})\cong\Ext_{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}({\boldsymbol{1}},{\mathcal{R}})\cong\Hom_{D_{\text{perf}}^{\check{G}}(S{\check{\mathfrak{g}}})}(S{\check{\mathfrak{g}}},\,S{\check{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes\Bbbk[{\check{G}}])\cong(S{\check{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes\Bbbk[{\check{G}}])^{\check{G}}\cong S{\check{\mathfrak{g}}}.

Thus, the functor
\Phi
may be identified with
a functor
{D_{{G}(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})}\to D_{\text{perf}}^{\check{G}}(S{\check{\mathfrak{g}}})
.
Furthermore, by
[
BFN2
, Lemma 5.13]
, this functor is an equivalence and
for any
{\mathcal{F}}\in{{\mathcal{P}}erv}_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})
one has

\Phi({\mathcal{F}})\cong{\mathscr{C}}\textit{h}^{*}(S{\check{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes\phi({\mathcal{F}}))
.
For
{\mathcal{F}}_{1},{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\in{{\mathcal{P}}erv}_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})
, writing
\Psi({\mathcal{F}}_{i})=V_{i}
, we compute

\displaystyle\Phi({\mathcal{F}}_{1})\otimes_{\sym{\check{\mathfrak{g}}}}\Phi({\mathcal{F}}_{2})

\displaystyle={\mathscr{C}}\textit{h}^{*}(S{\check{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes V_{1})\otimes_{\sym{\check{\mathfrak{g}}}}{\mathscr{C}}\textit{h}^{*}(S{\check{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes V_{2})

\displaystyle\cong{\mathscr{C}}\textit{h}^{*}(S{\check{\mathfrak{g}}}\otimes V_{1}\otimes V_{2})\cong\Phi({\mathcal{F}}_{1}\star{\mathcal{F}}_{2}).

Since the objects of
{{\mathcal{P}}erv}_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})
generate the derived Satake
category, it follows that the lax monoidal structure on
\Phi
is actually monoidal.

5.4.
Functoriality for the universal centralizer

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following
result.

Proposition 5.4.1
.

A morphism
\rho:H\to G
of connected reductive groups
induces a
morphism
{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{H}}\times_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}}J_{\check{G}}

\to J_{\check{H}}
of group schemes over
{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{H}}
.

If
\rho
is injective then the group scheme
J_{\check{H}}\to{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{H}}

has a natural
N_{G}(H)/H
-equivariant structure.

We will give two proofs of the proposition. The first proof
relies on the
description of the universal centralizer
J_{\check{G}}
in terms of the functor of
points given
[
DG
]
,
[
BFM
]
, cf. also
[
Ng1
, Proposition 2.2.3]
.
The second proof, which is well known, relies on geometric Satake.

First, we recall the construction of the functor of
points.
Let

\text{Sch}_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}}
, resp.
Ab
, be the category of schemes over

{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}={\check{\mathfrak{g}}}^{*}/\!/{\check{G}}
, resp.
abelian groups. Let
\Map_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}}({\mathfrak{t}}_{\check{G}}^{*}\times_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}}\text{-},T_{\check{G}})^{W_{\check{G}}}:\text{Sch}_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}}\to\text{Ab}
be the functor
that sends a test scheme
S\in\text{Sch}_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}}
to the abelian group
of
W_{\check{G}}
-equivariant morphisms
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\check{G}}^{*}\times_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}}S\to T_{\check{G}}
,
of schemes over
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\check{G}}^{*}
.
It is known that this functor is represented by an
abelian group scheme
J^{1}_{\check{G}}

smooth over
{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}
;
furthermore, the universal centralizer
J_{\check{G}}
is isomorphic to
an open subgroup scheme of
J_{\check{G}}^{1}
defined
by the condition that the group
\pi_{0}(Z_{\check{G}}(G))
maps surjectively onto

\pi_{0}(F)
for each closed fiber
F
of
J_{\check{G}}\to{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}
, cf.
[
Ng1
]
,
[
Ng2
]
.
Here,
\pi_{0}(\text{-})
denotes the set of connected components.

Given a subgroup
M\subseteq G
and subsets
S,S^{\prime}
of
G

we write
Z_{M}(S)=\{g\in M\mid gs=sg\ \forall s\in S\}
, resp.

N_{M}(S)=\{g\in M\mid gSg^{-1}\subseteq S\}
and

N_{M}(S,S^{\prime})=N_{M}(S)\cap N_{M}(S^{\prime})
.

Let
H
be a reductive subgroup of
G
,
and
T\subseteq G
a maximal torus such that

T_{H}=H\cap T
is a maximal torus of
H
.
The group
L:=Z_{G}(T_{H})
is a Levi subgroup of
G
.
Write

W=N_{G}(T)/T
, resp.
W_{H}=N_{H}(T_{H})/T_{H}
and
W_{L}=N_{L}(T)/T
, for the
respective Weyl groups.
It is easy to show that the group
W_{L;G}:=N_{G}(L,T)/T\subseteq W

equals the normalizer of
W_{L}
in
W
.

The following result is similar to
[
Ng2
, Lemme 10.1]
.

Lemma 5.4.2
.

Let
H\subseteq G
be a connected reductive subgroup.
Then, there is an isomorphism
N_{G}(L)/L\cong W_{L;G}/W_{L}
and
a natural group imbedding

W_{H}\,\hookrightarrow\,W_{L;G}/W_{L}
. Further, there is
a
short exact sequence

1\to W_{H}\to N_{G}(T_{H},T)/T_{H}\xrightarrow{q}N_{G}(H)/H\to 1,

which has a splitting provided by
a group homomorphism
s:N_{G}(H)/H\to N_{G}(T_{H},T)/T_{H}
.

Proof.

To prove the first statement, observe that
the kernel of the composite
N_{G}(L,T)\,\hookrightarrow\,N_{G}(L)\,\twoheadrightarrow\,N_{G}(L)/L

equals
N_{G}(L,T)\cap L=N_{L}(T)
. Thus, we get an injective homomorphism

W_{L;G}/W_{L}\cong N_{G}(L,T)/N_{L}(T)\,\hookrightarrow\,N_{G}(L)/L
.
For any
g\in N_{G}(L)
the torus

gTg^{-1}
is a maximal torus of
L
, hence one can find
\ell\in L
such that

gTg^{-1}=\ell T\ell^{-1}
. Hence
\ell^{-1}g\in N_{G}(L,T)
.
It follows that the
homomorphism
W_{L;G}/W_{L}\to N_{G}(L)/L
is an isomorphism.
Next, using that

N_{G}(T_{H})\subseteq N_{G}(Z_{G}(T_{H}))=N_{G}(L)
we deduce inclusions

N_{H}(T_{H})\subseteq N_{G}(T_{H},H)\subseteq N_{G}(L,H)\subseteq N_{G}(L)
.
The kernel of the composite
N_{H}(T_{H})\,\hookrightarrow\,N_{G}(L)\to N_{G}(L)/L
equals

N_{H}(T_{H})\cap L

=Z_{H}(T_{H})=T_{H}
. Therefore, the composite
induces an imbedding
W_{H}=N_{H}(T_{H})/T_{H}\,\hookrightarrow\,N_{G}(L)/L
, proving the second statement.

Similarly, the kernel of the composite
N_{G}(T_{H},H)\,\hookrightarrow\,N_{G}(H)\,\twoheadrightarrow\,N_{G}(H)/H
equals
N_{H}(T_{H})
,
so we get an imbedding
N_{G}(T_{H},H)/N_{H}(T_{H})\,\hookrightarrow\,N_{G}(H)/H
.
For any
g\in N_{G}(H)
the torus
gT_{H}g^{-1}
is a maximal torus of
H
.
Arguing as above, we deduce that the imbedding is a bijection. Thus, we obtain isomorphisms

(N_{G}(T_{H},H)/T_{H})/W_{H}\cong N_{G}(T_{H},H)/N_{H}(T_{H}){\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}N_{G}(H)/H.

This yields the short exact sequence of the lemma.

To complete the proof observe that the action of

N_{G}(T_{H},H)
in
{\mathbb{X}}^{*}(T_{H})
takes the set of roots of

H
to itself and takes Weyl chambers to Weyl chambers,
since it sends any Borel
B_{H}\supseteq T_{H}
of
H
to another such Borel.
Fix a Weyl chamber
C\subseteq{\mathbb{X}}^{*}(T_{H})
and let

N^{+}_{G}(T_{H},H)=\{g\in N_{G}(T_{H},H)\mid g(C)\subseteq C\}
.
Then,
N^{+}_{G}(T_{H},H)\cap N_{H}(T_{H})=\{1\}
and
the composite

N^{+}_{G}(T_{H},H)\,\hookrightarrow\,N_{G}(T_{H},H)\,\twoheadrightarrow\,N_{G}(T_{H},H)/N_{H}(T_{H})

is an isomorphism.
Inverting this isomorphism, we get a chain of
homomorphisms

N_{G}(H)/H{\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}N_{G}(T_{H},H)/N_{H}(T_{H}){\;\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\;}N^{+}_{G}(T_{H},H)\,\hookrightarrow\,N_{G}(T_{H},H)\,\twoheadrightarrow\,N_{G}(T_{H},H)/T_{H}.

The composite provides the desired splitting of the short exact sequence.
∎

First proof of Proposition
5.4.1
.

Let
\rho:H\to G
be a not necessarily injective morphism
of reductive groups.
Choose maximal tori

T_{H}\subseteq H
and
T\subseteq G

so that
\bar{T}_{H}:=\rho(T_{H})\subseteq T
.
There is a canonical map of Weyl groups

W_{H}=N_{H}(T_{H})/T_{H}\to W_{\rho(H)}=N_{\rho(H)}(\bar{T}_{H})/\bar{T}_{H}
.
We consider the Levi subgroup
L=Z_{G}(\bar{T}_{H})
of
G

and the dual Levi
\check{L}\subseteq{\check{G}}
.
We have
the identification
W_{\check{L}}=W_{L}
, resp.
W_{\check{G}}=W_{G}
and
W_{\check{H}}=W_{H}
. Thus,
we identify
W_{L;G}
with a subgroup
W_{\check{L};{\check{G}}}\subseteq W_{\check{G}}
.
In the setting of Lemma
5.4.2
we may (and will) replace
H
by
\rho(H)

and
let
\rho_{W}:W_{\check{H}}\to W_{{\check{L}},{\check{G}}}/W_{\check{L}}
be the composite
of the following maps

(5.4.3)

W_{\check{H}}=W_{H}\to W_{\rho(H)}\xrightarrow{\ \text{Lemma \ref{weyl lem}}\ }W_{L;G}/W_{L}=W_{{\check{L}},{\check{G}}}/W_{\check{L}}.

By construction, for all
w\in W_{\check{H}}
and
t\in T_{H}
, one has

\rho(w(t))=\rho_{W}(w)(\rho(t))
. We have the dual map

\rho^{*}_{\mathbb{X}}:{\mathbb{X}}^{*}(T)\to{\mathbb{X}}^{*}(T_{H})
, resp.
\check{\rho}:T_{\check{G}}\to T_{\check{H}}

and
\rho^{*}:{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}_{\check{H}}\to{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}_{\check{G}}
. Dualizing,
we deduce the equation

\rho^{*}_{\mathbb{X}}(\rho_{W}(w)(\lambda))=w(\rho^{*}_{\mathbb{X}}(\lambda))

for all
\lambda\in{\mathbb{X}}^{*}(T)
.
Hence, similar equations hold for
\check{\rho}
and
\rho^{*}
.

Given a test scheme
S\in\text{Sch}_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}}
and

f\in\Map({\mathfrak{t}}_{\check{G}}^{*}\times_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}}S,T_{\check{G}})

we form a chain of maps

{\mathfrak{t}}_{\check{H}}^{*}\times_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{H}}}S\xrightarrow{\,\rho^{*}\,}{\mathfrak{t}}_{\check{G}}^{*}\times_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}}S\xrightarrow{\,f\,}T_{\check{G}}\xrightarrow{\,\check{\rho}\,}T_{\check{H}}.

If the map
f
is
W_{\check{G}}
-equivariant
then it follows from the above that
the composite
\check{\rho}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}f

is
W_{\check{L};{\check{G}}}
-equivariant. Hence, for any

\lambda\in{\mathfrak{t}}_{\check{H}}^{*},\,s\in S
, and
w\in W_{\check{H}}
, we find

(\check{\rho}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}f{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}\rho^{*})(w(\lambda)\times s)=(\check{\rho}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}f)(\rho^{*}(\rho_{W}(w)(\lambda)\times s))=\rho_{W}(w)\big((\check{\rho}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}f{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}\rho^{*})(\lambda\times s)\big).

We conclude that the assignment
f\mapsto\check{\rho}{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}f{{}_{\,{}^{{}^{\circ}}}}\rho^{*}
yields
a morphism of functors

\Map({\mathfrak{t}}_{\check{G}}^{*}\times_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}}(\text{-}),T_{\check{G}})^{W_{\check{G}}}

\to\Map({\mathfrak{t}}_{\check{H}}^{*}\times_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{H}}}(\text{-}),T_{\check{H}})^{W_{\check{H}}}
, hence a morphism

{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{H}}\times_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}}J^{1}_{\check{G}}\to J_{\check{H}}^{1}
of group schemes over
{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{H}}
.
We must show
that this morphism
sends
{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{H}}\times_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}}J_{\check{G}}
to
J_{\check{H}}
.
By the description of
J
given above,
the map
\pi_{0}(Z({\check{G}}))\to\pi_{0}(F)
is surjective,
for every fiber
F
of
{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{H}}\times_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}}J_{\check{G}}\to{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{H}}
.
The action of
\pi_{0}(Z({\check{G}}))
in
F
agrees with
the restriction to the image of
the natural homomorphism
\pi_{0}(Z({\check{G}}))\to\pi_{0}(Z({\check{H}}))

of the
\pi_{0}(Z({\check{H}}))
-action
in the fiber of
J^{1}_{\check{H}}
that contains the image of
F
.
The first statement of the proposition follows from this.

To prove the second statement of the proposition we may
identify
H
with
\rho(H)
.
Then, the exact sequence of Lemma
5.4.3
shows that
the natural action of the group
N_{G}(T_{H},T)
on
{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}_{\check{H}}={\mathfrak{t}}_{H}

induces an action of
N_{G}(H)/H
in
{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{H}}
. Similarly, we obtain
an
N_{G}(H)/H
-action on the functor
\Map({\mathfrak{t}}_{\check{H}}^{*}\times_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{H}}}(\text{-}),T_{\check{H}})^{W_{\check{H}}}
.
It is straightforward to check that this
induces an action on the group scheme
J_{\check{H}}\to{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{H}}
.
∎

Second proof of Proposition
5.4.1
.

A morphism
\rho:H\to G
of
reductive groups induces an ind-proper
map
\rho_{\mathrm{Gr}}:{\mathrm{Gr}}_{H}\to{\mathrm{Gr}}_{G}
. Since
\rho^{!}_{\mathrm{Gr}}\omega_{{\mathrm{Gr}}_{G}}\cong\omega_{{\mathrm{Gr}}_{H}}
,
by adjunction one gets
an algebra morphism
H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{H(O)}(\omega_{{\mathrm{Gr}}_{H}})=H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{H(O)}(\rho^{\dagger}\omega_{{\mathrm{Gr}}_{G}})

\to\Bbbk[{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{H}}]\otimes_{\Bbbk[{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}]}H^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{G(O)}(\omega_{{\mathrm{Gr}}_{G}})
.
Thus, we obtain an algebra map

\Bbbk[J_{\check{H}}]\cong H^{{\textrm{\tiny{BM}}}}_{H(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}}_{H})\,\longrightarrow\,\Bbbk[{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{H}}\times_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}}J_{\check{G}}]\cong\Bbbk[{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{H}}]\otimes_{\Bbbk[{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{G}}]}\,H^{{\textrm{\tiny{BM}}}}_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}}_{G}).

One can check that this map respects the bialgebra structures and
the induced morphism

\rho^{*}J_{\check{G}}\to J_{\check{H}}
of group schemes over

{\mathfrak{c}}_{\check{H}}
is equal to the morphism from Corollary
5.4.1
.
Similarly, the action of
N_{G}(H)/H

on
H^{{\textrm{\tiny{BM}}}}_{H(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}}_{H})
induced by the
natural
N_{G}(H)
-action
on
{\mathrm{Gr}}_{H}
agrees with the
action of
N_{G}(H)/H
on

J_{\check{H}}
from Corollary
5.4.1
.
∎

6.
Proofs of main results

6.1.
Proof of Theorem
1.2.2

First we sketch the proof of the formality statement.
To this end, for each
\lambda\in{\mathbb{X}}_{+}
put

{\mathcal{R}}_{\leq\lambda}=\oplus_{\mu\leq\lambda}\,{\operatorname{IC}}({\overline{{\mathrm{Gr}}_{{\mu}}}})\otimes V^{*}_{\mu}
.
In
[
BF
, Sect. 6.5]
the authors constructed
an upgrading of the algebra

\Ext^{\>\raisebox{2.0pt}{\text{\circle*{1.5}}}}_{D_{G(O)}({\overline{{\mathrm{Gr}}_{{\lambda}}}})}({\mathcal{R}}_{\leq\lambda},\,{\mathcal{R}}_{\leq\lambda})

to a dg algebra. Using that these
\Ext
’s are pure
it was shown in
loc cit
that the constructed dg algebra is formal.
The derived Satake equivalence is deduced from this by passing
to a limit
D_{G(O)}({\mathrm{Gr}})=\underset{\,\longrightarrow\,}{\lim}\,D_{G(O)}({\overline{{\mathrm{Gr}}_{{\lambda}}}})
.

The technology of
\infty
-categories allows to package arguments in
loc cit

as follows. Fix a prime power
q
and an Artin stack
{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
with finite
stabilizers. For each
n\geq 1
let
{\mathcal{Y}}_{n}
denote the stack obtained from

{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}
by base change
to
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{n}}
. There is a (stable presentable)
\infty
-category

{\mathrm{Shv}}_{c}({\mathcal{Y}}_{n})
of constructible
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}
-sheaves
on
{\mathcal{Y}}_{n}
defined in
[
HL
, Sect. 4.1]
, cf. also
[
GL
]
. This category has a full subcategory

{\mathrm{Shv}}_{m,c}({\mathcal{Y}}_{n})

of mixed constructible sheaves, defined in
[
HL
, Sect. 4.2]
.
The category
{\mathrm{Shv}}_{m,c}({\mathcal{Y}}_{n})
comes equipped with an auto-equivalence

Fr^{*}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{n}}/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
given by the action of geometric Frobenius.
Finally, in
[
HL
